# Constituent Assembly of India (Legislative) Debates, 13 August 1948 — "Failure of Government to secure Protection against scaling down of Sterling Balances" (concluded), plus Starred Question on scaling down (FULL DAY TEXT)

**Source header**
- Corpus: Constituent Assembly of India (Legislative) Debates, Third Session, Volume VI, 1948 (9 Aug – 31 Aug 1948). 1947–1950 = Constituent Assembly of India (Legislative), NOT the Lok Sabha.
- Date of sitting: 13 August 1948
- Official publisher: Lok Sabha Secretariat / Parliament of India; Parliament Digital Library (eParlib)
  handle `https://eparlib.sansad.in/handle/123456789/760758`, bitstream `https://eparlib.sansad.in/bitstream/123456789/760758/1/cald_03_13-08-1948.pdf`
- **Route actually used — READ THIS (intermediary):** `eparlib.sansad.in` is unreachable from this
  session (the egress gateway answers 403 to CONNECT for `eparlib.nic.in` / `archive.org` alike, and
  all direct `curl` is blocked). The text below was obtained from the **Internet Archive mirror** of
  the official eParlib scan — IA item `archive.org/details/eparlib.nic.in.760758`, OCR derivative fetched at
  `https://archive.org/cors/eparlib.nic.in.760758/cald_03_13-08-1948_djvu.txt` — retrieved via the Firecrawl MCP `firecrawl_scrape` tool.
  This is therefore an **Internet Archive Tesseract-OCR rendering of the official printed volume**,
  NOT a direct read of the Sansad website and NOT a read of the printed page. OCR errors are
  frequent and visible (digits are especially unreliable — leading "1"s are routinely dropped, so
  "1949" often prints as "949" and "£1,537 million" as "£,537 millions"; running-head years are
  sometimes wrong). Substantive prose is legible. **Any figure quoted from this file must be
  re-checked against the printed volume before publication.**
- Retrieved: 2026-08-17
- Keyword counts across this whole file (case-insensitive, whole file including index/contents):
  sterling 122 · Britain 16 · British 99 ·
  London 12 · balances 67 · agreement 34

- **Why this document matters:** contains (i) a Starred Question by Shri B. P. Jhunjhunwala
  asking Government whether it will take a categorical assurance from the British Government that
  the question of scaling down the sterling balances will never arise, with the Finance Minister's
  answer; and (ii) the concluded cut-motion debate, including a long speech by **Shri T. T.
  Krishnamachari** — the same man who, nine years later as Finance Minister, moved the amendment
  that made the Foreign Exchange Regulation Act permanent.

The allotment of Units and Sub-Units of the Senior and Junior Division to Colleges

ए79, *Shri B. Shiva Rao: Will the Honourable Minister of Health be 
Pleased to state: 


(a) the amount of financial assistance given by Government to the Nutrition 
Research Institute at Coonoor per year; 

(b) the activities undertaken by the Institute; and . 

(०) the steps taken by Government to publish the results of research 
carried out by the Institute? 


The Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookerjee: (a) The Nutrition Researoh 
Leboratories, Coonoos, ६८७ not financed directly by the Government of India, but 
by the Indian Research Fund Association which controls the Laboratories. The 
financial allotments sanctioned during the three yeara for the Nutrition Research 
Laboratories, Coonoor, are: 

(i) For the year 94647-Re. ,26,58. 
(ii) or the year 947-48-Re. ,87,42. 
(iti) For the year 3948-49-Rs. ,87,20 


(b) The Nutrition Research Laboratories, Céonoor, carry out basio researoh 
on various aspects of the nutrition problenr pertaining to human nutrition and 
food. Short courses of instruction on nutritisn “both for medical and non- 
medical men are conducted by the Laboratories. The advice of the Director on 
probleme of nutrition is also made available to Central and Provincial Govern 
ments and other organisations when askéd for. 

(9) The report of the work carried out at the Nutrition Research Laboratories 
is printed und published in the annual report of the Scientific Advisory Board 
of the Indian Research Fund Association and in the annual report of the Nutri- 
tion Research Laboratories. Scientific investigations carried out by the mem- 
bers of the staff are often published in the Indian Journal of medical Research 
and in other scientific journals. During the years 9l9—946 (both inclusive) 
no less than 29] scientific communications were published. One _ informative 
pamphlet on the nutritive value of Indian foods, another one on the food value 
of rice and a third one in popular Tanguage for children on the subject of food 
and nutrition. have been prepared by the Coonoor Lahoratoriea and published by 
.the Government of India in the Health Bulletin series. हि] 

Shri K. Santhanam: May I know if it. ie a fact that the Nutrition Research 
Institute has been in a state of stagnation for sometime now and has made no 
progress? 

The Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookerjee: The information at our 
disposal is quite ७८४ the contrary. 

Shri K. Santhanam: May I know whether there has been anv expansion in 
the activities and the staff of the Institute since it was founded? 3 

The Honourable Dr. Syamsa Prassd Mookerjee: Several new appointments 
have been made and I would refer the honourable member to the leet report 
published for 947-48. ‘ 

Pandit Lakahm! Kantsa Maitra: May I know whether besides official publica- 
tions the results of the research done in the Institute have been widely publisiced 
"3७ the country, 50 as to make them available to the publio at large? 


232 OONSTITUENT ASSEMBLY oO INDIA (LEGISLATIVE) [l3TH avoust 7948 


Tne Honourable Dr. Syama Prassd Mooberjeo: Perhaps thst cap be con- 
sidered. The results of the Institute have been published in technical journals 
and I would refer the honourable member to the last report of the Institute for 
the year 947.-48 published this year. 

Pandit Lalabmi Kanta Maitra: Will the Honourable Minister aleo kindly 
eee that these-results are widely publicised through the radio ७० that the ‘in 
formation is readily available to the public? 


Tne Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookerjee: The question of giving wider 
publicity to the results of the Institute can be considered. 

Shri T. A. Ramalingsm Chettiar: Are the Government aware that to the 
people nothing-is-known-of the results obtained by the Research Institute. 
There are several such institutes working both under the provincial governmonts 
and the Central Government. Ie there any attempt made to co-ordinate the 
activities of these institutes and published the results for popular use? 

The Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookerjl: The reports are published 
from time to time and they are made available to those who are interested in 
them. There has been some co-ordination. sormetimee we receive requests from 
provincia! governments or other institutions which are doing research work or 
Pursuing similar activities. 

Shri H. V. Kamath: Does the research carried on at the Institute bear any 
relation to the diet that the poorest in the country can afford? 

The Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookerjee: Naturally, that muet be one 
of the considerations. 

Shrims Ammu Swaminadban: May I mow if the reporte of the Institute 
are published only in English or sre also published in the languages of the 
different provinces ? 

The Honourable Dr, Syams Fraesd Mookerjes: I am airaid the reports ere 
published mainly in English. 

Prot. N. ७, Bangs: Some of them are published in Hindi. 

Bbr 8, Shiva Bao: Are Government aware that the activities of this 
Institute are extremely valuable and the Institute itself ie considered the finest 
research institute in the whole of Asia, despite the niggardly attitude of the 
Government towards it? 

The Hononrable Dr. Syama Prassd Mookerjee: Honourable Member will 
please define the word ‘‘niggardly”’. 

Prof. N. ७. Bangs: How many workers are being trained every yeer m this 
Institute? 

The Honourable Dr. Sysma Prasad Mookerjee: Last year the total number, 
I believe, wae forty. 

Prof. व. G. Ranga: Will Government consider the advisability of instituting 
a diploma course for getting people trained in thie Institute, in view of the fact 
that this Institute is becoming one of the world’s most famous institutions? 

The Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookerjee: That might be considered.’ 

Shri 8, P. Jhunjhunwala: Do the nutrition bulletins which are being issued 
by the Government contain anything which affect the livee of the masses of the 
villagere ? 

The Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookerjee: They contain valuable <ug- 
gestions which if given effect to will improve the condition of the masses. 

Shri) Alagemn: Is it not a fact that the result of tlie research con- 
ducted in this Institute were published in the Harijan und were even commented 
upon by Mahatma Gandhi? 


STARRED QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS 233 


Shri B.-Shiva Bao: Is there any intention on the part of the Government of 
giving thie Inetitute accommodation for itself instead of its being dependent 
upon the Pasteur Institute? 

The Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookerjee: The question of improving 
the accommodation for the institute will be considered. 

Babu Bamnarayan Singh: May I know what ia the way in which the lives 
of the people in the villages are being influenced by the results of the activities 
of this Institute? 

‘Mr. Speaker: That has been replied to. There have been so many questions 
om that aspect and they have been answered. 

Shri 5, 9, Jhunjhunwala: May I know if the bulletins thst are issued at 
present are of any use to the masses? Is it not a fact that they cannot be 
applied to them under existing conditiona?

389, *Dr. Bakhshi Tek Chand: Will the Honourable Minister of Health be

(a) whether it is a fact that the ouly medical educational institution in

in Lahore:

Punjab at Amritear, which is insufficient to meet the requirements of that

jovernment of India realise the urgent necessity of establishing an up- 
to-date Medical Coliege in Delhi; and है

The Honourable Dr, Syama Prasad Mookerjee: (a) Yes.

regarding the establishment at Delhi of an All Fodia Medical Institute, of

students two additional seats have been reserved at the Glancy Medical College,

been arrange in the Medical College at Jaipur. ‘I'he Government of India

93. *Shri B. P. Jhunjhunwala: (a) Will the Honourable Minister of 
Finance be pleased to state whether the attention of Government has been 
drawn to the reply given by Sir Stafford Cripps to a question put by Mr. 
‘Winston Churchill regarding the question of the scaling down of sterling 
balances that this question wi:l always be open for discussion? 


(b) If 80, do Government consider the des'‘rability of taking a categorical 
assurance from the British Government that the question of scaling down of 
sterling balances will never arise? 


The Honourable Shri R. EK. Shanmukham Chetty: (x) and (b). The «atten- 
tion of Government has been drawn to the.reply given by the Chancellor of the 
Exchequer to a question put by Mr, Winston Churchill regarding the question 
‘of scaling dowa of Sterling Balances. The question put. by Mr. Churchill 
relates tu the Defence expenditure during the war. It wi.] be seen from para- 
graph 2 of the letter from Sir Henry Wilson Smith to Mr. A. K. Chanda that 
tbe payment. of £55 million made by the British Government to the Govern- 
ment of India will be in full and final settlement between the two Govern- 
ments and the Government of Pakistan on all matters arie‘ng out of the 
operation of the Defence Expenditure plan. The construction that the Gov- 
ernment of [ndin place upon this paragraph of the letter is that the whole 
question of the allocation of the Defence expenditure has been s*tt’ed once for 
all and is not oven for further discussion. The Government of India are not 
‘bound by any statements made in Parlirment which are not in  sccordance 
with the terms of the agreement contained in the various letters exchanged 
between the two Delegations. In view of this position, the statement ef the 
Finance Minister at the Press Conference that the ghost of acaling down has 
been हि lnid, is correct.

22l, *Srijat Rohini Kumar Chaudhuri: (a) Will the Honourable Minister of 
Health be pleused to state the total number of persons attacked by plague in- 
Calcutta, Bombay and Madras during the period let January to Both June 
948 and how many of the ९886९ proved fatal? 

(b) What are the reasons for the sudden outbreak of plague in the Ins§- 
spring and eummer in Calcutta? 

. . (¢)..What are the steps taken by Government for the prevention and oure 
of this disease? 

The Honourable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookejee: (a) A statement is laid on the 

. table of the House. ५ 

(०) It is considered that the origin of the recent outbreak of pingue in 
Calcutta. lics in an Epizootic which Probably occurred about January this year, 
The epizootic probably occurred by the introduction of germs into the rat popula- 
tion from outside through merchandiao from other infected localities and alao 88 
8 result of the reduction of immunity in the rat population. 

(0) As soon as the report about plague in Calcutta came to the knowledge of 
the Government of India, the Deputy Director General of Health Services and 
some other officers were specially deputed to visit Calcutta to help tho Provincial 
authorities to deal with the situation. Large eupplies of plague vacciue a8 
D.D.T., which in many cases had to be flown by plane, were also made available 
by Government to the Director of Health Services, Weat Bengal. On the 
advice of the Deputy Director General of Health Services other urgent measures 
taken by the Provincial authorities to bring the epidemic under yuick control 
were (i) a mage inoculation of the public, (ii) rat destruction, (iii) disineectisation 
of houses, (iv) proper disposal of garbage, sind (v) setting up an Isolation Hospital 
specially for the treatment of plague cases.

PayMENT oF PENsIONs IN U. हू. 


2i4. *Shri R. R. Diwakar: Will the Honourable Minister of Finance be 
pleased to xtute 


(a) the advantages of the purchase of tapering annuities for payment of 
pensions in the U.K. instead of the payment of a capitalised sum; 


(b) whether, in the determination of thie obligation, any hereditary or 
perpetual pensions have been taken into consideration; and 


(¢) if 80, what was the amount involved and who had granted these per- 
petual pensions? 


STARRED QUESTION AND ANSWERS 257 


The Honourable Shri R. K. Shanmukham Ohetty! (a) to (0). Tho pensionary 
liability hos been capitalised in the sense that in return for a lump sum pay- 
ment ‘nade te the British Government that Government will make available to 
the Government of Indis tupering annuities for payment of the pensions in 
England. If the amount of the annuity payable any year is not sufficient to 
meet the charges for that year, the difference will be found by the Government 
sof India. Conversely, if the amount of the annuity is in excess of the payment 
for any one year, such excess will be appropriated by the Governtnent of India. 


Though the pensiorary linbility has been capitalised. us exptained above, the 
responsibility for the payment of pensions bas not been transferred to the 
British Government. The Government of India are of the opinion that the res, 
ponsibility for the payment of these pensions should be in their hands and that 
iunder any administrative arrangement that may be made the British Govern. 
ment should aot only as their agents. 


The amount of the finance required from year to-year was determined on a 
lump sum basis and not with reference to individual pensions.

MINES AND MINERALS (REGULA'TION AND DEVELOPMEN'!') BILL 


The Honourable Shi N. V. Gadgil (Ministcv for Works, Mines and Power): 
Sir, I beg to move for leave to introduce : Bill to provide for the regulation of. 
miyes and oilfields and for the duvelopnmicut of minerals. 

Prof. N. 9. Rangs (Madras: Getieru!) Some time ago the Government of 
India hod annouaced their policy and stated that mines are on the list of tbose 
industries: which should be nationalised immediately, and I am rather surprised 
that just as they had done in the case of the Electricity Bill, they have come 
forward with some sort of a thing and ask for the regulation of mines and 
oilfields, thug preventing anyhow for some years to come any chance of 
nationnlising the mincs, I think the Honse deserves an explanation. 

Mr. Speaket: The honourable member will note that the present motion ls 
merely one for leuve for introduction, a formal sort of thing on which ordinarily 
there is no objection or no discussion, though, of course, both are permissible 
but in practice we do 00६ do so. I put the motion to the House. 

The question is: 

“That leave be granted to inireducs a Bill to provide for tho regulation of mines and 
‘eilficlds and for the development of minerais.”’ 

The motion was adopted. 

The Honousable Shri N. V. Gadgil: Sir, I introduce the Bill.

USMANDS FOR SUPPLEMENTARY GRANTS. 948-49 267 


Dr. P. 8, Deshmukh (C.P. and Berar: General): Mr. Speaker. Sir wo arc all 
very much thankful to you, Sir, for having «llotted sufficient time for the 
disoussion of this very important subject. We have already had yesterday ns 
many as 4 speeches. The first was by ry honournble friend Mr. Lari, who 
ofiticised the whole arrangement, aud he was also followed by equilly critical 
honourable members like Prof. Shibban Lal Sakeens and Prof. K. T. Shah. 
Prof. Saksens, Sir, almost told us that we were overtaken by a deluge, that 
everything was lost, that for generatjona to come we were undone, and there 
was, according to him no hope of resurrection. Prof. i. T. Shab, Sir, na we know 
bas always been feeling very strongly on the auhicct, and if the Elouve will 
Tecollect the speech that he made on the same subject some months back, it was 
quite clear that he was very much apprehensive of scaling down of our Sterling 
Balances. His previous speech was followed yesterday by o deolarntion that 
what had happened ‘was highly disappointing nnd that India’s interests had 
been irrevocably injured. All these speakers, Sir, took a view which was 
bordering on the alarmist and there are many members of the Houre also who 
share this feeling. J for one, Sir, think that the position is not as hail »s hax 
bean reflected in the speeches made by these members and that it is possible 
to take a somewhat more constructive view of the whole matter. One thing 
I agree with what came from Prof. K. Tf. Shah and that is, that in my opinion, 
there is adequate ground to feel that the approach of our Delegation towards 
the subject was not altogether satisfactory. Prof. K. T. Shah was quite corrvct 
in stating that while we were very sympsthetic, verv conridernte towards tho 
difficulties of the British Government. we should not have forgotten the bad 
times through which we ourselves were passing. We ourselves arc facod with 
food shortage, with our industries producing less and less, with increased 
poverty of our people, with inflation and so many other factors which are 
contributing to greater and greater unhappiness of the people. In fact, 2 lot 
of people quite correctly ask whether tho Independence that we have zot has 
really contributed to the intrinsic happinesa of the people. We are suffering 
in so many different ways ond ail thia anffering of the people should have 
weighed with the Delegation ३०-७४ to press the British Government and to say: 
Of course youf difficulties are great, but our difficulties are greater: we ara & 
nation who have emerged into Independence newly; we are in a somewlint 
precarious condition’, and our difficulties sbould have been made to weigh 
with the British Government far more then has been the oase. That ie a line 
of thought, Sir, with which I nm in the fullest agreement, and while we should 
and we might show any sympathies and consideration for the difficulties of 
Sir Stafford Cripps and his Government, we ought not to forget that our con- 
dition was far more precarious and from that. point of view it should have heen 
possible for our Delegation to get at. least rorne better tenns than we have 
geoured. The Honoursble Minister bad to admit, Sir, our failure in not being 
able to utilise the Sterling Balances that were available last year. It will 
eonvince nobody in the House to say that some other pert of the Govarninend 
has erred and it is no consolation to anybody; the mistake is there that we 
have not been able to utilise the Sterlieg Balances that were available and 
if we continue in the same manner, we will be throwing opportunities that 
were available to us. We could ask the Finance Miniater as to what per- 
suaded him to tie down the interests of (ndia for auch a length of time a 6) 
. It we look to the schedule given in tho-white paper, we wil! 600, Gir. 

that the vayments are going to be made for as long o neriod as 80 yearn 


Tho सकल» Shri B. K. Shanmukham 07490 (Mininter for Finance): 
‘That is purely en sctusrial calculation, ‘There ia no tying down of the Govern- 
went of India. It is a calculation of the probable yeat when the last pensioner 


will die. 
De. P. 8, Dughmakb: I am prepared to correct myself, Sir, that we have 


8 CONSTITOENT ASSEMBLY OF INDIA (LEGISLATIVE) [l4TH avGUsT i948 
not been tied for so long, but I would like to know for what length of time we 
are going to be dependent upon the British Government to recover all the 
Sterling Balances that they owe us. As every one of us knows for the 
Present we are asked to he content only with l84 million punde. May I ask 
Sir, at this rate, how many years it is going to take us to realize all that 
Britain owes us? Is there any estimate that has been framed? Hae it been 
placed before the House as to how many years it is going to take ro as to 
receive back all the payments that Britain is expected to make? 


There is alsaanother point. which I would like to place before the House. 
Since the accumulated balances come to euch a _ high figure 
as 586 crores, when wt know that we are deficient in so many things, we 
have yet to have even a semhlance of a navy, we have not got an air force ab 
our command, I should like to ask whether it war not possible to put this 
to the British Government. in o direct way. We all know, that inspite of the 
war losses the British Navy continues to be tbe largest in the world even 
today. The British people and the Britieh Government are not also deficient 
in the manufacture or possession of aeroplanes. There are many other articles, 
raany other capital goods which could have been made available to us for our 
utilisation within a shorter period than appears to have been contemplated by 
the delegation. Sir, in my view, it is quite correct to complain that we have 
not pressed our point of view 88 earnestly as we should and if we have 
really pressed it all that we wanted to press, we have not, at 
any 7806 succeeded to the extent this House would have liked. After all, the 
world situation, as everybody knows, is grave. We do not know what may 
happen within a period of’ six months hence. So, when an agreement or 
nrrangement that ir placed before us is so strikingly indefinite as not to be 
able to disclose when we would be really getting all that we have sacrificed 
for—because, what is owed to us by the British Government was not what 
we could have easily apared—the accumulation of sterling balances was at the 
eost of the happiness of the people of India and is money which India could 
ill-afford to let lie with the British Government, 88 has heen now disclosed at 
8 rate of interest which is abnormally low-—the House ie certainly entitted to 
complain against the delegation and to say that India has not been well trented 
by Britain, nor have you secured, although there may’ he difficulties of which 
we may be conscious, terms with which we could feel ahsolutely satisfied. £ 
thiuk, Sir, that line of criticiam is entirely justifiable. I would like to ask 
the Finance Minister whether the same situation that arose last year, unmely 
our inability to take ndvantage of even.the aterting balancee* that are made 
available to_us, to be repented and whether he bus not planned and placed 
before the British Government other adequate means of securing within the 
shorteat possible period the sterling balances due from Great Britain. I would 
also ask him whether it was not porsible to ask them to part with some of 
their ships, aeroplanes and machinery which they may be probably utilising at 
the present moment but of which Yn: die stands in grester need of. I would alao 
like him to state whether it was not fossible for the delegation to obtain some 
goods at once so that wo could be satisfied that a substantial amount out 
of thie huge eum of sterling halancea was immediately made available to us. 


So far as the pensions affair is concerned, 3 think the delegation does 
deserve our compliments that they did not allow themselves to be persuaded 
to pay the whole capitalised value in a lump sum. There is the question of our 
having to wait for a number of years and therefore it je probably wise that we 
should not pay up anything that is owed by us to Great Britain m the shape 
of pensions, all at once: That may be a very sound argument to consider;

In regard to stores ] think the boat case that has beeu made for this settle- 
ment wae made by Dr. Pattabhi Sitaraimayya. He said that the disposal ot 
stores by our Government in the market was made for just a song. Actually 
the stores are sold at a price fixed by the department. ‘I'be prices fixed by us 
in regurd to the payment in England has been on that basis. A tiu of preserved 
eatables was sold for just about 4 or 2 annus; and if our black-marketeers 
made money on that, somebody benefited in India, no one in -England did. 
Actually it happens that so far as disposal stores are couce! the vulue was 
an extremely conservative one and they accepted it. We did not try to puil 
wool over their eyes, we tried to be very fair and we had to tell them that we 
had to guard the interests of the Indian exchequer and take uo risks. My own 
feeling is, as @ person who has gone closely into it along with the officers of 
our deegation, that it is not a bad job. I will uot say it is a bargain; we did 
not try to cheat them of what was due to them but we did guard ourselves 
against possible loss and we have amply guarded ourselves; though it happened 
that some of our friends on the Pakistan side who were with us did probably 
tell the people there that India was trying to drive & hard bargain. We were 
very fair and, incidentally, you can take it that we have been honourable.

the details. I think I have rather been treading on dangerous ground in talk- 
ing about military stores and I will not go deeper. But l can assure this House 
that while we have not: tried to oheat Britain into any transaction which is 
unfavourable to her we have amply guarded ourselves against any ppssible 
contingency of @ loss, And I think therefore, the criticism made against the 
settlement in this regard is extremely unfair. There has been ४ large volume of

werned, the criticiam ia really off the maxk.- 


One word about the settlement in regard to pensions. In this House it 
would have been observed that there has been a wide divergence of opinion 
in regard to the settlement of pensions. Some ople felt 
that what was done was the best method of settling, some peaple: felt, that 
we should have made an outright settlement straightaway and given U.K. a 
fixed amount and got rid of the whole businesa of paying pensions thereby 
finishing it off lock, stock and barrel. In fact I will not he revealing any 
secret when I say that there was difference of opinion even in the delegation 
on this matter. Personally I felt that we had better get rid of this responsibi- 
lity once for all und be done with it, so that we wiil not have to maintain a staff 
to pay there pemsious and we will not have anv annuities and any annual 
adjustments to make in our budget which perplex my honourable friend Mr. 
Das. But the majority of the delegates thought otherwise and tht Govern- 
«ment of India eupported their view. There is much to he said on both sides. 
The point. in favour of this type of pension settlement has been urged hy the 
Finance Mimister. One or two additional pointe I should like to mention. If 
we were to settle the whole thing lock, atock and barrel at one time there would
